# 2510.14502v1_Additive_Density_Regression

## Page 1


Additive Density Regression
Eva-Maria Maier1, Alexander Fottner2, Sonja Greven3,*, and Almond
St¨ocker4,*
1,3 Humboldt-Universit¨at zu Berlin, Berlin, Germany
2 Augsburg University, Augsburg, Germany
4 EPFL, Lausanne, Switzerland
*The last two authors, listed in alphabetical order, share senior authorship.
October 17, 2025
Abstract
We present a structured additive regression approach to model conditional den-
sities given scalar covariates, where only samples of the conditional distributions
are observed. This links our approach to distributional regression models for
scalar data. The model is formulated in a Bayes Hilbert space – preserving non-
negativity and integration to one under summation and scalar multiplication –
with respect to an arbitrary finite measure. This allows to consider, amongst
others, continuous, discrete and mixed densities. Our theoretical results include
asymptotic existence, uniqueness, consistency, and asymptotic normality of the
penalized maximum likelihood estimator, as well as confidence regions and in-
ference for the (effect) densities. For estimation, we propose to maximize the
penalized log-likelihood corresponding to an appropriate multinomial, or equiv-
alently, Poisson regression model, which we show to approximate the original
penalized maximum likelihood problem.
We apply our framework to a moti-
vating gender economic data set from the German Socio-Economic Panel Study
(SOEP), analyzing the distribution of the woman’s share in a couple’s total labor
income given covariate effects for year, place of residence and age of the youngest
child. As the income share is a continuous variable having discrete point masses
at zero and one for single-earner couples, the corresponding densities are of mixed
type.
Keywords: Bayes Hilbert Space; Distributional Regression; Structured Additive
Model; Penalized Maximum Likelihood Estimation; Mixed Densities; Density-
on-Scalar Regression.
1

1
Introduction
We consider a regression setting with pairs of scalar response and covariate vector ob-
servations (yi, xi), i = 1, . . . , N. Classical regression approaches model the mean of
the conditional response distribution given the covariates xi. However, it can be very
restrictive to account only for dependencies in the mean (and no further characteris-
tics) of, typically, parametric distribution families. Kneib et al. (2023) in their ‘Rage
Against the Mean’ review different “distributional regression” approaches that over-
come this limitation by modeling the whole conditional response distribution. They
focus on generalized additive models for location, scale and shape (also sometimes
called distributional regression, e.g., Rigby and Stasinopoulos, 2005; Klein et al., 2015)
working with parametric distribution families, conditional transformation models and
distribution regression modeling the cumulative distribution function (cdf) of the re-
sponse (e.g., Hothorn et al., 2014), quantile and expectile regression (e.g., Koenker,
2005; Newey and Powell, 1987), and an approach (e.g., Dunson et al., 2007) modeling
conditional response densities as a mixture of densities of some known distribution fam-
ily, with parameters as well as mixture weights depending on the covariates (extended
in Rodr´ıguez-´Alvarez et al. (2025) to structured additive regression models for the mix-
ture means). While these methods allow for considerable flexibility, Kneib et al. (2023)
point out that: “One particular difficulty encountered frequently when starting to apply
distributional regression to a given data set is the challenge of interpreting the resulting
regression effects. In particular, all models discussed in this review require the analyst
to move beyond the simple and convenient ceteris paribus type of interpretation, where
the effect of differences in one covariate of interest can be interpreted while keeping all
other covariates fixed” (Kneib et al., 2023, Section 7).
We aim to fill this gap, introducing flexible structured additive regression models
for conditional response densities that allow for ceteris paribus interpretation of es-
timated covariate effects, while also developing inference and providing an implemen-
tation via our R package DensityRegression (developer version on https://github.
com/Eva2703/DensityRegression). Modeling the density (instead of say quantiles or
the cdf) allows for straightforward interpretations and visualizations of bimodalities or
shifts of probability mass depending on the covariates, and paves the way for future
extensions to bi- or multivariate distributions.
In addition, our approach does not
require an assumption of (mixtures of) known parametric distributions.
To respect nonnegativity and integration to one, we consider densities as elements
of a Bayes Hilbert space (Egozcue et al., 2006; van den Boogaart et al., 2014) – an
extension of the Aitchison geometry for compositional data (Aitchison, 1986). The
type of densities is specified via a reference measure, in particular allowing for mixed
continuous-discrete densities (as well as the common continuous and discrete special
cases). Those occur in our motivating gender economic application, where we ana-
lyze the woman’s share in a couple’s total labor income in Germany depending on the
couples’ location in (West or East) Germany, its child status and the year: While the
conditional densities of the woman’s income share can be assumed to be continuous on
(0, 1) for double-earner couples, they exhibit point masses at 0 and 1 reflecting single-
earner couples. In addition to their mixed structure, the conditional densities of the
woman’s income share are subject to further modeling challenges (Maier et al., 2025):
2


## Page 3


The continuous component of such densities is known to be multi-modal for some co-
variate combinations, which requires flexible nonparametric modeling. Moreover, the
categorical and continuous covariates require different types of partial effects in the
model (group-specific, smooth, and interactions). Finally, it is of primary interest to
interpret such effects on the women’s income share distribution, an established object
of gender economics (e.g., Bertrand et al., 2015; Sprengholz et al., 2022), rather than to
only estimate/predict conditional distributions. Our approach is designed to account
for these challenges: Formulating our structured additive regression models in Bayes
Hilbert spaces allows for mixed densities and (odds ratio) ceteris paribus interpretation
of effects building on Maier et al. (2025); representing the partial effects using a tensor
product basis, building on spline bases for mixed densities developed in Maier et al.
(2025) as well as appropriate bases for the covariate effects, allows for flexible model-
ing of the shapes of the densities (in particular covering multimodality) and various
types of partial effects. The spline coefficients are estimated via penalized maximum
likelihood estimation. We show that the resulting estimator is asymptotically normally
distributed and construct confidence regions and p-values for the estimated effect den-
sities. As the corresponding likelihood contains an integral term making maximization
computationally expensive, we show that the likelihood, the penalized maximum like-
lihood estimator (PMLE), and its estimated covariance can be approximated via an
appropriate multinomial, or equivalently, Poisson regression model obtained by binning
the continuous component of the mixed density.
Interestingly, it turns out that modeling densities in Bayes Hilbert spaces – effectively
corresponding to a Hilbert space structure on the log-density-level – together with
maximum likelihood-based estimation is closely related to conditional logspline den-
sity estimation (e.g.,
Stone, 1991; Gu, 1995), despite coming from a very different
perspective. Stone (1991) consider (unpenalized) maximum likelihood estimation for
logspline density estimation, where continuous conditional densities given one con-
tinuous covariate are modeled as the normalized exponential function of a predictor
given by a B-spline tensor product basis. For identifiability, they choose a sum-to-
zero constraint on the coefficients, similar to the integrate-to-zero constraint arising in
the Bayes Hilbert space framework. In subsequent work, Stone (1994) also consider
multivariate conditional densities with multiple covariates. Both manuscripts focus
on theoretical results, deriving optimal rates of convergence, not on the (complicated)
computation of the maximum likelihood estimator in practice nor on interpretation or
inference. Mˆasse and Truong (1999) address more practical problems for the setting of
Stone (1991), proposing to use a modified Newton algorithm to compute the (unpenal-
ized) maximum likelihood estimator. They give pointwise confidence bounds, pointing
out, however, that resulting bands are problematic as they do not only contain den-
sities. Gu (1995) propose penalized maximum likelihood estimation for continuous or
discrete (and also multivariate) densities given scalar covariates, also based on the lo-
gistic density transform. They model the predictor via a (trimmed) analysis of variance
(ANOVA) decomposition in a reproducing kernel Hilbert space, which is determined
by the roughness functional used for penalization. The ANOVA components are then
modeled via tensor product smoothing splines, where the observations serve as knots,
estimated via a Newton algorithm.
Regarding identifiabilty, Gu (1995) propose to
set some value “averaging” the modeled functions to zero, which our integrate-to-zero
3


## Page 4


constraint is an example of. They derive rates of convergence for the estimator of the
conditional densities, but no asymptotic distribution and (thus) no confidence regions
or p-values. Such conditional logspline density estimation approaches are effectively
covered as special cases by our density regression framework (except for differences
in estimation details). The formulation in a Bayes Hilbert space adds, in particular,
a novel more principled perspective on density modeling, facilitating new modeling
flexibility well beyond previous models, practical ways of estimation, valid statistical
inference, and means of (odds ratio-type ceteris paribus) interpretation. At the same
time, we are able to draw on some of the previous theory.
Binning procedures for (conditional) density estimation have been previously used: Eil-
ers and Marx (1996, Section 8) discuss histogram smoothing for estimating continuous
densities (without covariates), while Gao and Hastie (2022) base conditional density
estimation via boosted trees effectively on a Poisson histogram model, which they refer
to as “Lindsey’s method” (Lindsey, 1974; Lindsey and Mersch, 1992). Our binning of
the continuous density component’s support is related, while we add a formal proof of
convergence.
Bayes Hilbert spaces have been used to model conditional densities before (Maier et al.,
2025; Jeon and Park, 2020; Talsk´a et al., 2018). Different from this paper and all of
the above citations, these approaches belong to the class of “distributional response
regression”. Such methods model the mean distribution/density given covariates with
respect to a metric on probability distributions – here the Bayes Hilbert space metric,
in other cases, e.g., the L2 metric after log-quantile/-hazard transforming the densities
(Han et al., 2020) –, typically assuming whole probability distributions/densities to be
observed rather than samples thereof (with a few exceptions working with empirical
distributions using an optimal transport metric, Zhou and M¨uller (2024), or in Bayes
Hilbert spaces, Jeon and van Bever (2022)). In the common case of only samples being
available, these methods thus require a two-step procedure, first estimating the “density
observations” from the sample, e.g., via kernel density estimation, before the actual
analysis. Such a two-step approach is particularly problematic if only few observations
of each conditional density are available, which is usually the case in the presence of
continuous covariates. Also, it carries the well-known problem of zero observations
in parts of the domain (compare, e.g., Pawlowsky-Glahn et al., 2015) for the case
of distributional response regression in Bayes Hilbert spaces. We provide a natural
solution for these problems in the following by proposing a one-step approach that
models the latent conditional density building on Maier et al. (2025), while fitting the
model on observed individual observations.
Our structured additive model framework formulated for densities in a Bayes Hilbert
space avoids a two-step approach by working with samples of the conditional distri-
butions, but marries distributional regression and distributional response perspectives,
and yields a sound, flexible, and practically applicable framework, which to the best
of our knowledge is unmatched by existing methods: 1) Our models allow for a variety
of types of covariate effects (e.g., categorical, linear, smooth, interactions), which can
be, 2), interpreted ceteris paribus. 3) Penalties may be added to obtain regularization.
4) We provide a unified framework for different types of densities (including discrete,
continuous, and mixed ones). 5) Showing asymptotic normality, we derive valid con-
fidence regions and p-values on the level of densities. 6) We reduce the non-standard
4


## Page 5


estimation problem for mixed (and continuous) densities to an additive Poisson model
over bins, showing asymptotic equivalence for decreasing bin sizes. 7) We provide an
implementation in our own R package DensityRegression, building on the numerous
specification possibilities and estimation efficiency of the well-known R package mgcv
(Wood, 2017) for estimating our Poisson models.
Our structured additive density regression model and estimation approach are intro-
duced in Section 2, together with our theoretical results. In Section 3, we use our
method to analyze female income share distributions, before presenting a simulation
study based thereon in Section 4 and concluding with a brief summary and discussion
in Section 5.
2
Additive Density Regression
In this section, we present our penalized maximum-likelihood approach for estimating
conditional densities from random samples. Since densities are viewed as elements of a
Bayes Hilbert space, we first introduce these spaces in Section 2.1, before formulating
our structured additive density-on-scalar regression model and deriving the correspond-
ing log-likelihood function in Section 2.2. We show that the PMLE asymptotically ex-
ists and is unique, consistent and asymptotically normally distributed. Furthermore,
we construct confidence regions. In Section 2.3, we show that the log-likelihood, the
PMLE and the penalized Fisher information can be approximated using a multinomial
or equivalently Poisson distributional assumption. Finally, we discuss interpretation of
estimated effects in Section 2.4. Proofs are provided in appendix A.
2.1
Bayes Hilbert spaces
We briefly summarize Bayes Hilbert spaces, following our more detailed introduction
in appendix A of Maier et al. (2025) based on van den Boogaart et al. (2010) and van
den Boogaart et al. (2014). These manuscripts also provide proofs for all statements.
Consider a measure space (Y, A, µ), where µ is a finite measure called reference mea-
sure. Denote the set of σ-finite measures with the same null sets as µ by M(µ) =
M(Y, A, µ). Then, there exists a µ-almost everywhere (µ-a.e.) positive and unique
density with respect to µ (Radon-Nikodym derivative) for every measure in M(µ).
The addendum “µ-a.e.” is omitted in the remaining work for the sake of readability.
We thus can identify each measure in M(µ) with its density. Note that densities in
M(µ) not necessarily integrate to one. However, proportionality with respect to pos-
itive constants defines an equivalence relation ∝on the densities in M(µ). We call
the corresponding set of ∝-equivalence classes Bayes space (with reference measure µ)
and denote it by B(µ) = B(Y, A, µ). For an equivalence class [f] ∈B(µ) containing
densities with finite integral, the respective probability density f/

While Bayes Hilbert spaces are defined for arbitrary measurable spaces (Y, A) as long as
the reference measure µ is finite, we focus on Y ⊂R with three common cases (Maier et
al., 2025): A continuous Bayes Hilbert space corresponds to Y = Yc = [a, b] for a, b ∈R
with a < b, where A = B is the Borel σ-algebra restricted to Yc, and µ = λ is the
Lebesgue measure. For a discrete Bayes Hilbert space, we have Y = Yd = {t1, . . . , tD}
with A = P(Yd) the power set of Yd and µ = δ := PD
d=1 wdδtd a weighted sum of Dirac
measures with wd > 0 for all d = 1, . . . , D. A mixed Bayes Hilbert space combines
both of the aforementioned cases, with Y = Yc ∪Yd, A chosen to be the smallest
σ-algebra containing all closed subintervals of Yc and all points of Yd, and µ = δ +λ. If
Yd ⊂Yc, we obtain A = B. Allowing either Yd or Yc to be empty includes continuous
and discrete Bayes Hilbert spaces as special cases, thus it suffices to consider mixed
Bayes Hilbert spaces in the following.

Here,
Pj = ξX, j(PX, j⊗IKY)+ξY, j(IKX, j⊗PY) penalizes the j-th partial effect with smoothing
parameters ξX, j, ξY, j ≥0 in the respective directions, j = 1, . . . , J, and P is the

The smoothing parameters can be determined, e.g., by restricted maximum likelihood
(REML) optimization (Wood, 2017, Section 6.5.2). In the following, the special case
P = 0 (corresponding to ξX, j = 0 = ξY, j for all j = 1, . . . , J), yielding the unpenalized
log-likelihood, is always included, the considered model is assumed to be identifiable,
and basis functions and penalty matrices are chosen as proposed above (basic properties
necessary for the validity of the theorems/lemmas but fulfilled for standard basis choices
are given in appendix A.1.2). Note that then, ℓpen is defined on RK.

cussion and derivation of (i) and (ii). Note that, for N ≥N ′, assumption (BX) is
furthermore implied by assumption (BX’), which itself is fulfilled, if the observations of
the covariates are spread reasonably over their domain with growing sample size (com-
pare assumption (BX”) in appendix A.1.3 for the formal statement), see Lemma A.5 2).
Moreover, note that (BX) is equivalent to the nonsingular-condition for logspline re-
gression models (e.g., Stone, 1991), while assumptions (BX’) and (BX”) are inspired
by (1) and (2) of Stone (1991). Finally, regarding assumption (S<), note that under
assumption (BX), for every v ∈SK−1 there exists an i ∈{1, . . . , N} such that sv,xi
is not constant (Lemma A.3) and thus, the assumption can be fulfilled. Furthermore,
under assumption (BX”), not only does (BX) hold, but also the probability that (S<)
holds tends to one for N →∞, see Lemma A.5 3). The following theorem then yields
asymptotic existence of the PMLE ˆθ.
Theorem 2.1. Under (BX), ˆθ is unique, if it exists. If (S<) holds as well, ˆθ exists.
Asymptotic existence is also contained as byproduct in the following Theorem 2.2
on consistency and asymptotic normality. While these two properties of the PMLE
are well-known for “regular log-likelihoods”, this term is often not specified further
in the literature, particularly in our case of non-identically distributed (but inde-
pendent) observations.
In contrast, we clearly state all necessary assumptions and
provide proof in appendix A.2, which also includes a brief discussion of literature.

2.3
Relation to Multinomial and Poisson Regression
The integral term in (2.3) makes maximizing ℓpen computationally expensive and re-
quires specialized implementations. We thus show that ℓpen(θ) and Fpen(θ) can be
approximated by a (shifted) penalized log-likelihood/Fisher information obtained from
multinomially distributed data, which is constructed from the original response obser-
vations y1, . . . , yN by combining all observations of the same conditional distribution
(i.e., all observations sharing identical values in all covariates) into a vector of counts
via a histogram on Yc \ Yd and counts on Yd. We show convergence for decreasing
bin width and that this also transfers to the PMLEs under mild assumptions. Fur-
thermore, we derive that Poisson regression can be used equivalently to multinomial
regression. This is very appealing, since Poisson regression is well-known and flexible
additive implementations are available. Thus, in our R package DensityRegression
we build on mgcv (Wood, 2017) for fitting Poisson models.
To formally construct the multinomial data, let x(1), . . . , x(L) ∈X be the unique com-
binations of observed covariate values and let I(l) := {i ∈{1, . . . , N} | xi = x(l)} for
l = 1, . . . , L ≤N denote the corresponding partition of indices. Thus, {yi | i ∈I(l)}
forms an independent and identically distributed random sample of Y (l) | x(l). Note that
in the presence of continuous covariates, we usually have distinct observed covariate val-
ues, i.e., I(l) = {l} for l = 1, . . . , L = N, with each observation sampled from a different
conditional distribution. In the following, let l ∈{1, . . . , L}. The set I(l) is partitioned

predictor should be chosen in such a way that the coefficients θ (and intercepts τ (l) in
the Poisson distribution assumption) are identifiable. When using Theorem 2.6 for our
regression approach, we choose the predictor as η(l)

Theorems 2.5 and 2.6 imply that we can use Poisson regression to estimate the pa-
rameter vector in ℓpen and thus build our approach’s implementation on existing ones.
Furthermore, justified by our theorems, we may replace ˆθ with ˆθ

pen)−1)[K] in Lemma 2.3 to construct confidence regions in practice.
Note that there might be numerous unique covariate combinations, in particular if
metric covariates are involved.
Thus, the number of intercepts τ (l) to estimate for
l = 1, . . . , L in the Poisson regression model can get very large.
In practice, this
problem might be circumvented by modeling the intercepts smoothly in the covariate
basis as well, i.e., τ (l) = bX(x(l))⊤τ, where τ ∈RKX . However, as in any additive
Poisson regression model like here for the absolute counts n(l), one needs to carefully
inspect the counts to decide whether this smoothness assumption is reasonable.
In the beginning of this section, we constructed one vector of counts per unique observed
covariate combination x(l), l ∈{1, . . . , L}, combining all observations of the same
conditional distribution. However, the construction described above can also be carried
out for partitions of the corresponding set of indices I(l), without changing estimation
results, compare Lemma A.16 in appendix A.2. In the most extreme case, this yields
one vector of counts per observation (with one count of 1 and all others 0). While this is
usually not desirable with respect to computation time in practice, since it inflates the
size of the data set artificially, it shows the estimation does not depend on aggregation
of observations and the presented approach is thus overall coherent.
2.4
Interpretation
An advantage of formulating our structured additive regression models in Bayes Hilbert
spaces is that it allows for ceteris paribus interpretation of partial effects as proposed by
Maier et al. (2025). In the following, we briefly summarize two of their interpretation
methods. For this purpose, let hj = hj(x) ∈B2(µ), j ∈{1, . . . , J} be a partial effect
as in model (2.1) and h0 = h0(x) ∈B2(µ) the sum of the remaining partial effects,
omitting the covariates x ∈X in the notation for the sake of readability.
For the first interpretation method, consider the infinitesimal odds ratio ORj(s, t) :=
(h0(s)⊕hj(s))/(h0(t)⊕hj(t))
h0(s)/h0(t)
= hj(s)/hj(t) for s, t ∈Y. As then log ORj(s, t) = clr[hj](s) −
clr[hj](t), the log odds ratio can be easily read off as vertical differences from plots of
the clr transformed effects. As ORj(s, t) is independent of h0, these odds ratios can
13


## Page 14


be interpreted ceteris paribus. In addition to the infinitesimal interpretation, Maier
et al. (2025, Proposition 3.1 (b)) show that ORj(s, t) corresponds to the limit of the
usual odds ratio in the vicinity of s and t. Furthermore, Proposition 3.1 (a) in Maier
et al. (2025) implies that if ORj(s, t) > 1 for all s ∈A, t ∈B, where A, B ∈{C ∈
A | µ(C) > 0}, we have P(A)/P(B) > P0(A)/P0(B) where P and P0 denote the
probability measures corresponding to the densities h0 ⊕hj and h0.
The second approach examines shifts of probability masses under summation: For
α ∈R, let A+ := {hj ≥α} and A−:= {hj < α}. Then,
R
A+ h0 ⊕hj dµ ≥
R
A+ h0 dµ
and
R
A−h0 ⊕hj dµ ≤
R
A−h0 dµ (Maier et al., 2025, Theorem F.1), i.e., any threshold α
decomposes Y into two sets – one where the probability mass of any density h0 ∈B2(µ)
increases under perturbation with hj and one where it decreases.
Note the result
also holds, when constructing A+ and A−based on clr[hj] instead of hj, since the clr
transformation retains a density’s monotonic behavior.
3
Application
The density of the woman’s share in a couple’s total labor income is considered in
several gender economic contributions (e.g., Bertrand et al., 2015; Sprengholz et al.,
2022). A brief discussion thereof is available in Maier et al. (2025), who (in contrast to
the aforementioned gender economic literature) analyze the dependence of such income
share densities on covariates. Their density-on-scalar regression approach requires pre-
liminary estimation of densities from samples of the conditional distributions, which
then serve as response densities for the regression. Our newly developed method avoids
this two-step approach by directly working with the samples and additionally provides
inference for the effect functions. In the following, we apply our modeling approach to
the same data set considered by Maier et al. (2025), which is derived from the German
Socio-Economic Panel (SOEP) (version 33, doi:10.5684/soep.v33, see Goebel et al.,
2019). It contains N = 154, 924 observations of opposite-sex couples living together in
one household (not necessarily married), with at least one partner reporting positive
labor income.
We aim to model conditional densities of the woman’s share s in a couple’s total
gross labor income, given three covariates. The binary covariate West East indicates
whether the couple’s household is located in West or East Germany (including Berlin).
The covariate c age categorizes the couples via the age of the youngest child living in
their household into three groups: 0-6 (zero to six years), 7-18 (seven to 18 years),
and other (no minor children). The third covariate year ranges from 1984 for West
Germany and from 1991 for East Germany to 2016. Given these covariates, we consider
the conditional densities fWest East, c age, year : [0, 1] →R+ of the share s as elements of
a mixed Bayes Hilbert space B2(µ) = B2([0, 1], B, µ) with reference measure µ :=
δ0 + λ + δ1, since the boundary values 0 and 1 of the share, corresponding to single-
earner households, have positive probability mass. We estimate the model
fWest East, c age, year = β0 ⊕βWest East ⊕βc age ⊕βc age, West East
⊕g(year) ⊕gWest East(year) ⊕gc age(year)
⊕gc age, West East(year),
(3.1)
14


## Page 15


where all summands are elements of the Bayes Hilbert space B2(µ), i.e., densities of s.
We use reference coding with the same reference categories as Maier et al. (2025),
namely West East = West, c age = other, and year = 1991 (with the latter, for
a continuous covariate, not directly specifiable in mgcv’s function gam(), which our R
package DensityRegression uses for fitting Poisson models, requiring a transformation
as discussed in appendix B). The intercept β0 corresponds to the density given these
covariate values. The other effects then describe the deviation from the reference for
different values of the respective covariate, e.g., the smooth effect g(year) gives the
deviation for each year from the reference 1991 (for West Germany and c age other).
The model also contains several interaction terms, namely, a group-specific intercept
βc age, West East as well as group-specific smooth effects gWest East(year), gc age(year), and
gc age, West East(year). Note that more covariates might be included (at the expense of
longer computation time), however for comparability purposes we consider (almost)
the same model as in Maier et al. (2025). The only difference of model (3.1) to theirs
is that they include an additional group specific intercept corresponding to a fourth
covariate region, which contains a finer disaggregation of the covariate West East into
a total of six regions. Since they discovered almost no variation within the regions
corresponding to West and East Germany, respectively, we decided to not include this
additional covariate for the sake of model parsimony. The basis functions bY ∈B2(µ)KY
are obtained from cubic B-splines for the continuous and indicator functions for the
discrete component as described in Section 2.2. Like Maier et al. (2025) we use no
penalty, setting ξY, j = 0 for all j = 1, . . . , J. Since their two-step approach includes
pre-smoothing by estimating the response densities, the number of basis functions used
in the actual regression model is not comparable and we use a lower number (KY = 15)
to obtain a similar level of smoothness. For the (group-specific) smooth effects, we
use cubic B-splines with second order difference penalty for bj and Pj and REML
optimization to determine the smoothing parameters ξX, j (given in appendix D.1). For
histogram construction on (0, 1), we use G = 100 equidistant bins for each covariate
combination.
Note that the data set contains a sample weight per household, which we basically
incorporate by using weighted counts as response data. However, these sample weights
and thus the weighted counts are not natural numbers, technically not valid as response
observations in a Poisson model. Thus, we construct an equivalent log-likelihood by
weighting the respective log-likelihood contribution with the quotient of the weighted
and the unweighted count and subtracting the quotient’s logarithm from the predictor.
See appendix C for details.
For the level α = 5% (even for α = 1%), all effects except for the group-specific
smooth effects gWest East(year) and gc age, West East(year) are significant, compare the
(simultaneous) p-values shown in Table D.2 in appendix D.2. There, we also give p-
values which are pointwise in the covariates in Table D.3. In the following, we discuss
the main effects of model (3.1). All effects are shown in appendix D.3, in particular the
interaction effects, for which we cannot go into detail here, due to space constraints. In
Figures 3.1 to 3.3, the left side illustrates the sum of the intercept and the respective
main effect (i.e., the conditional densities given the different values of the considered
covariate and the reference values for the remaining covariates), while the right side
shows the clr transformed effects. The latter is useful for interpreting effects via log
15


## Page 16


odds ratios or shifts of probability mass, see Section 2.4. Note that for the discrete
values 0 and 1, the density values are visualized as dashes and shifted slightly outwards
for better distinction.
The estimated effect of West East is shown in Figure 3.1 via the colored lines. The
grey ones illustrate samples (of size 100) from the respective 95% confidence regions
constructed as in Lemma 2.3 (simultaneous over Y = [0, 1] and point-wise regarding
the covariates). Note that ˆβ0 ⊕ˆβWest = ˆβ0 and clr[ˆβWest] = 0 due to reference coding.
Considering double-earner couples (s ∈(0, 1)) in the left part of the figure, we observe
both estimated conditional densities have a mode around 0.45. For couples in West
Germany (without minor children in 1991) the density has a tendency towards a second
mode around s = 0.1. For the former communist East Germany, there is a shift of
probability mass to the right yielding a more symmetric density. This is also indicated
by clr[ˆβEast] on the right: Using the value α ≈−0.65, we obtain that the probability
mass of the estimated densities in East Germany decreases on {clr[ˆβEast] < α} =
(0, 0.2) compared to West Germany (ceteris paribus). Regarding the boundary values,
nonworking women (s = 0) are more frequent in West than in East Germany, while
single-earner women (s = 1) are more frequent in East Germany. We can quantify
this via the log odds ratio of ˆβEast and ˆβWest for s = 1 compared to t = 0, which is
clr[ˆβEast](s)−clr[ˆβEast](t)−(clr[ˆβWest](s)−clr[ˆβWest](t)) = 0.23−(−0.09) = 0.32, which
means that the odds for single-earner versus nonworking women in East Germany are
exp(0.32) ≈1.38 times the odds in West Germany. The (clr transformed) densities
sampled from the simultaneous (over [0, 1]) confidence regions show similar shapes to
the estimated densities over large parts of the domain, in particular regarding our
findings. However, towards the boundaries, i.e., for s →0 and s →1 with s ∈(0, 1),
the functions spread out, reflecting estimation uncertainty due to few observations in
these areas.

17

sampled from the confidence region have similar shapes as the estimated ones with dis-
persion towards the boundaries. A color gradient and different line types indicate the
different years. Considering the left part of the figure, in each year, the density (for
couples without minor children living in West Germany) reaches its maximum around
s = 0.45 with a second mode around s = 0.1 developing over time. Furthermore, the
estimated densities tend to disperse within s ∈(0, 1) over time, with probability mass
particularly increasing for low positive income shares, while the share of nonworking
women (s = 0) decreases. This is a sign of women switching from no paid work to
part-time employment, emphasizing the need for considering mixed densities, since an
analysis of double-earner couples only (s ∈(0, 1)) might misinterpret this development
as a shift from larger to smaller income shares. Moreover, the frequency of single-earner
women (s = 1) increases over time, in the most recent years reaching a similar level as
the (strongly decreasing) frequency of nonworking women (s = 0). The right part of
the figure also indicates the increasing dispersion on (s ∈(0, 1)), as the clr transformed
effects tend to be smaller for low and high income shares (e.g., s ∈A = (0, 0.2)∪(0.8, 1))
than for income shares in between (e.g., t ∈B = (0.2, 0.6)) before the reference year
1991. This reverses afterwards. Hence, the odds of the probabilities of A (outer area)
versus B (central area) are smaller in earlier than in later years. This implies that over
time the probability of A increases and the probability of B decreases.

All in all, our new approach finds similar effects as the one of Maier et al. (2025), but by
working directly with the samples of the conditional distributions more appropriately
uses the available information and additionally provides uncertainty quantification.
Note that we also illustrate and briefly discuss all estimated conditional densities in
appendix D.4, again confirming findings of Maier et al. (2025) for their pre-smoothed
density-observations and predictions.
4
Simulation study
In this section, we present a simulation study to validate our approach.
We focus
on the setting of our application with the estimated conditional densities obtained in
Section 3 serving as true conditional densities fWest East,c age,year, where West East ∈
18


## Page 19


{West, East}, c age ∈{0-6, 7-18, other}, and year ∈{1984, . . . , 2016} for West East =
West and year ∈{1991, . . . , 2016} for West East = East. We consider four different
sample sizes, N ∈{50000, 150000, 500000, 1000000}, where 150000 corresponds to the
rounded sample size of the data set underlying the application, and three different
bin numbers for the continuous component, G ∈{50, 100, 200}, using equidistant bins
that are equal for all covariate combinations. We thus omit the superscript indicating
the covariate combination in the notation compared to Section 2.3. For i = 1, . . . , N,
the i-th covariate combination xi = (West Easti, c agei, yeari) is drawn with equal
probabilities from all possible covariate combinations. Regarding the sampling of the
women’s income share si ∈[0, 1], note that due to the binning conducted to create the
count data, the exact value within one bin of the continuous component is not relevant
and it suffices to draw si from the set of bin midpoints united with the discrete values
{0, 1}, i.e., from {u1, . . . , uΓ}, where Γ = G+2. The probability of sampling ug is given

20


## Page 21


The empirical coverage rates for predictions (as constructed in Lemma 2.3) and main
effects (as constructed in Lemma A.15, i.e., simultaneous in covariates) are illustrated
in Figure 4.2, where the green vertical line indicates the true theoretical value α = 95%.
Appendix E contains the analogous figure showing all effects (Figure E.2), as well as
empirical coverage rates for partial effects based on point-wise confidence regions as
constructed in Lemma 2.3 (Figure E.3). For predictions, the range of the empirical
coverage over the 177 conditional distributions mostly reduces and converges to the
theoretical value 95% with increasing N. The only exception is the combination of
smallest bin number, G = 50, with largest sample size N = 1000000. While the me-
dian improves slightly, from 92.5% to 93% compared to N = 500000, the range also
increases, possibly indicating that too-small bin numbers may induce undercoverage es-
pecially for larger sample sizes, where variance decreases and even small approximation
biases thus may be non-negligible. Similarly, for the intercept, the empirical coverage
rate for the largest sample size N = 1000000 is worst for G = 50, only 89%, while cover-
age rates are close to the theoretical value with 94.5% (G = 100) and 93.5% (G = 200)
for more bins, and only slightly below the coverage rates of 95% (G = 100) and 94%
(G = 200) obtained for N = 500000. For the group-specific intercepts ˆβWest East and
ˆβc age the empirical coverage rates are close to the true value. For ˆg(year), we observe
some undercoverage for the smallest sample size N = 50000 (again most severe for the
smallest bin size: 87.5% for G = 50, 89% for G = 100 and 89.5% for G = 200), while
for larger values of N, there is slight overcoverage. This trend is similar for the inter-
action effects ˆgWest East(year) and ˆgc age(year) (with even more severe undercoverage
for N = 50000), while for the three-way interaction ˆgc age, West East(year), we observe
slight overcoverage already for the smallest sample size, N = 50000 (compare Fig-
ure E.2). The results based on point-wise confidence regions illustrated in Figure E.3
are similar, with empirical coverage rates (mostly) close to the theoretical value for
(group-specific) intercepts (with sometimes slightly worse results for G = 50 compared
to G ∈{100, 200} given the same N), while the (group-specific) smooth effects of year
yield more varied coverage rates (over the different years) for the smallest sample size,
N = 50000, contracting around 95% (or a larger value for the three-way interaction)
with increasing N.
All in all, the smallest considered sample size, N = 50000, yields large relMSEs in
some iterations – mostly for βc age, which exhibits a sharp drop on clr-level – and tends
to yield some undercoverage for predictions and year-effects. The available data size
in the application, N = 150000, already yields good results – especially given the
complexity of the considered model with several interaction effects. Estimation quality
unsurprisingly improves further with more data available. Regarding the bin number
G, we observe almost no influence on the relMSE values within the reasonable ranges
considered here, while the empirical coverage rates in some cases suffer for the lowest
bin number compared to good coverage for the larger two considered bin numbers.
5
Conclusion
We presented a penalized maximum-likelihood approach to estimate structured addi-
tive regression models for densities given scalar covariates from random samples of the
underlying conditional distribution. To respect their nature, densities are considered
21


## Page 22


as elements of a Bayes Hilbert space B2(µ), providing a unified framework for arbi-
trary finite measure spaces, in particular including mixed densities (and discrete and
continuous special cases), which we focused on for estimation. We derived the asymp-
totic distribution of the penalized maximum likelihood estimator (PMLE), which we
used to construct confidence regions and p-values for estimated partial effects. Our
framework is the first distributional regression approach to allow ceteris paribus inter-
pretations for covariate effects. Furthermore, we approximated the log-likelihood by
a multinomial or equivalently Poisson log-likelihood, with the respective observations
computed via histograms on the continuous and counts on the discrete part of the
domain, and showed that the PMLE and its estimated covariance (the inverse Fisher
information) converge to the ones of the limiting (Bayes Hilbert space) log-likelihood.
This simplifies estimation, allowing to take advantage of the well-established R package
mgcv (Wood, 2017) for our implementation in the R package DensityRegression. We
used the new additive density regression approach to analyze the motivating (mixed)
densities of the woman’s income share for couples in Germany, observing similar co-
variate effects as Maier et al. (2025), but avoiding a two-step estimation approach and
additionally providing inference. In particular, we found significant main effects for
West vs. East Germany, presence vs. absence of minor children in the household, and
time. A simulation study based on our application further validated our approach.
Formulating our regression models directly for densities in a Bayes Hilbert space not
only allows to consider continuous, discrete, and mixed densities in a unified framework,
and to include a variety of covariate effects, but also to interpret these effects ceteris
paribus. Thereby, our approach stands out from other distributional regression ap-
proaches modeling the conditional distribution based on individual observations, which
lack such interpretability (Kneib et al., 2023). In contrast, distributional response re-
gression approaches benefiting from the Bayes Hilbert space framework (e.g., Talsk´a et
al., 2018; Maier et al., 2025) assume whole densities to be observed as response objects,
which is usually not possible and thus necessitates a two-step procedure. This leads to
additional estimation uncertainty and requires enough observations to estimate con-
ditional densities for each covariate combination, which typically cannot be expected
in the presence of continuous covariates. Our maximum-likelihood approach modeling
the conditional densities directly based on the individual observations overcomes these
issues. In addition to these strong advantages, the Bayes Hilbert space framework also
has limitations in certain settings. As Bayes Hilbert spaces contain (µ-a.e.) positive
densities, densities that are zero on parts of the domain cannot be modeled. How-
ever, our new estimation approach based on individual data avoids zero density values
that often occur in two-step approaches due to presmoothing (or over-presmoothing
to prevent zero values), providing a natural solution to this problem except in settings
with structurally different density supports. Furthermore, the reference measure needs
to be finite, excluding the Lebesgue measure as reference for Y = R. However, the
probability measure of a reference distribution, such as the standard normal distribu-
tion, provides an alternative reference in this case (van den Boogaart et al., 2014), and
the Lebesgue measure can be used for the finite domain case commonly observed in
practice.
In contrast to existing literature (e.g., Mˆasse and Truong, 1999), we provided simulta-
neous confidence regions, containing complete densities. The asymptotic distribution
22


## Page 23


of the PMLE these are based on was derived assuming a true parameter value to exist
for the given finite-dimensional basis expansion. As the MLE is known to converge un-
der standard assumptions to the minimizer of the Kullback-Leibler divergence within
the modeled class of distributions, here spanned by the considered basis (Knight, 1999;
Stone, 1991), our semi-parametric conditional distribution estimates can be interpreted
as best approximations to the true conditional distributions within a finite but po-
tentially high dimensional model space. Given the large model flexibility compared
to existing approaches, and similarly to other penalized spline approaches (Ruppert,
2002), this should not be much of a limitation in practice. Future work, motivated
by Stone (1991), might also study convergence and inference as the number of basis
coefficients goes to infinity. Furthermore, we aim to extend the presented approach
from univariate to multivariate densities in future.

APPENDIX
A
Proofs
This section contains proofs for all statements formulated in our main manuscript. We
start with preliminaries in Section A.1. More in detail, in Section A.1.1, we introduce
some notation and show a first auxiliary statement (Lemma A.1), which is used in
the following subsections. In Section A.1.2, we specify necessary properties of basis
functions and penalty matrices. All of them are actually very common and in par-
ticular fulfilled for our suggestions in Section 2.2. Thus, these properties are always
assumed to hold, without mentioning them explicitly in statements afterwards. In Sec-
tion A.1.3, we recall the assumptions introduced in Sections 2.2 and 2.3 and specify the
additional assumption (BX”), which was only briefly described, but not given explic-
itly in Section 2.2 as it requires further notation (introduced in Section A.1.1) and is
rather lengthy. Furthermore, Section A.1.3 contains a more detailed discussion of the
assumptions and proof of the statements made in Sections 2.2 and 2.3 regarding the
assumptions (Lemma A.5). Auxiliary statements, which are used several times in later
proofs, are collected in Section A.1.4. Finally, Section A.2 contains the proofs for all
theorems/lemmas of our main manuscript, and also Lemma A.15, where we construct
confidence regions and p-values, which are simultaneous in the covariates (and over
Y), as mentioned in Section 2.2 after Lemma 2.3. Note that the proof of Theorem 2.2
is rather extensive and requires several auxiliary statements (namely, Lemmas A.11,
A.12, and A.14), which are not used in any other proof. Thus, they are arranged after
the proof of Theorem 2.1, in the same section as the proof of Theorem 2.2. This section
also contains a brief discussion of the proof strategy and literature before formulating
and proving the auxiliary statements.

Proof. Since the functions ¯bT , 1, . . . ,¯bT , KT +1 ∈L2(λ) are linearly independent and the
transformation matrix ZT has full rank, the transformed basis ˜bT , 1, . . . ,˜bT , KT ∈L2
0(λ)
consists of linearly independent functions (Liesen and Mehrmann, 2015, Lemma 9.23).

in ˜bT are elements of span(¯bT ). Since 1T /∈span(˜bT ), the functions ˜bT , 1, . . . ,˜bT , KT , 1T
are linearly independent.

i.e., span(¯bT ) = span(˜bT ,1, . . . ,˜bT ,KT , 1T ).

tion IX, (36) B-spline Property (iv)).
Since the functions contained in ˜bYc all in-
tegrate to zero, we have 1Yc /∈span(˜bYc). The basis of indicator functions ¯bT , 1 =
¯bYd,1, . . . ,¯bT , KT +1 = ¯bYd,KYd+1 fulfills the linear independence assumption and 1Yd ∈
span(¯bYd) straightforwardly, while 1Yd /∈span(˜bYd) follows analogously with the inte-
grate-to-zero constraint. Thus, in both cases the assumptions are fulfilled.
A.1.2
Necessary Properties of Bases and Penalties
For penalty matrices and basis functions not chosen as in Section 2.2, we assume:
(P) All penalty matrices Pj are positive semi-definite, j = 1, . . . , J.
(SPP) There exist R ∈N and κ ≥2 such that every function contained in span(˜bYc)
is a (κ −1)-times continuously differentiable piecewise polynomial consisting of
R polynomials of degree at most κ.
(B) The basis functions contained in ˜bY as well as the basis functions contained in
bX are linearly independent, continuous in any yc ∈Yc \ Yd respectively in any
x ∈X, and bounded.
Since these are very common properties of penalty matrices/(spline) basis functions,
these are not mentioned explicitly in the remaining work to not overload the statements.
Now, consider the choices of penalty matrices and basis functions recommended in
Section 2.2, included in an identifiable model.
We briefly discuss, that the above
assumptions are fulfilled:
3


## Page 29


• (P): As marginal penalty matrices PX, j, j = 1, . . . , J, and PY in Section 2.2, we
propose to use difference penalties and identity matrices, which are positive semi-
definite.
Since Kronecker products and linear combinations (for non-negative
scalars) of positive semi-definite matrices are positive semi-definite (see Horn and
Johnson, 1994, Corollary 4.2.13, and Horn and Johnson, 2012, Observation 7.1.3,
respectively), Pj = ξX, j(PX, j ⊗IKY) + ξY, j(IKX, j ⊗PY) is positive semi-definite
for all j = 1, . . . , J i.e., assumption (P) is fulfilled.
• (SPP): Let κ ≥2 denote the degree of the (unconstrained) B-splines contained
in ¯bYc. Every function contained in span(¯bYc) is a (κ −1) times continuously
differentiable piecewise polynomial with the breakpoints corresponding to the
knots defining the B-spline basis (De Boor, 2001).
By Lemma A.1, we have
span(˜bYc) ⊂span(¯bYc) and thus every function contained in span(˜bYc) is also
such a piecewise polynomial and we obtain (SPP).
• (B): We first consider ˜bY. In the continuous or discrete special case, linear inde-
pendence of the functions contained in ˜bY was already shown in Lemma A.1. In
the mixed case, the basis in L2
0(µ) is obtained by applying the embedding ˜ιc given
in (A.1) to the linear independent functions ˜bYc, 1, . . . ,˜bYc, KYc ∈L2
0(λ) and the em-

d, 1, . . . ,˜bY•

0(δ•). Since these embeddings are injective, they retain linear independence and
thus the functions contained in the vector ˜bY are linearly independent. Conti-
nuity and boundedness of the functions contained in ˜bY follows per construction
from continuity and boundedness of the B-spline basis ¯bYc, 1, . . . ,¯bYc, KYc+1.
Regarding bX, the linear independence, continuity, and boundedness assumptions
are fulfilled for usual choices of basis functions bX, j, j ∈{1, . . . , J}, in particu-
lar the ones we recommend in Section 2.2 (recalling the covariate domain X is
compact). Note that across different partial effects, i.e., for j ̸= j′ ∈{1, . . . , J},
identifiability constraints ensure linear independence, where necessary (e.g., if
both partial effects are an effect of the same smooth covariate – one a main
effect, the other an interaction effect). Hence, assumption (B) holds.

In the following remark, we discuss these assumptions, amplifying the respective dis-

Remark A.2.
1) We will show in Lemma A.5 1) that assumption (BX) holds, if
BX has rank KX. Here, we thus focus on fulfilling this rank condition, which can
actually be achieved by choosing the basis functions bX, j, j ∈{1, . . . , J} appro-
priately for given observations x1, . . . , xN. More precisely, for j ∈{1, . . . , J}, the
basis functions bX, j have to be chosen such that
(i) their number KX, j is at most the number of unique observed values of the
subvector of covariates, on which the j-th partial effect depends,2
(ii) all functions contained in bX, j have at least one observation in their support
(using B-splines to model smooth effects of continuous covariates).
Let BX, j ∈RN×KX, j denote the matrix containing bX, j(xi) in the i-th row.
First we derive that under conditions (i) and (ii), BX, j has rank KX, j. To see
this, note that the rank of BX,j equals the number of linearly independent rows,
which is at most the number of unique rows. Thus, (i) ensures that KX, j is at
most the number of unique rows. The second condition (ii) then ensures that
BX, j has linearly independent columns, or equivalently, rank KX, j. For a linear
effect of a continuous covariate or a group specific intercept of a grouping vari-
able this is straightforward. For smooth effects modeled via a B-spline basis see
De Boor (2001, Section XIV, Lemmas (23) and (25)).3 Note that in practice,
these bases might be transformed to enforce necessary identifiability constraints.
Per construction this retains linear independence of columns in BX,j for each
2The subvector might contain only one covariate. If it depends on more than one covariate, the
number of unique observed values of the subvector corresponds to the unique observed combinations
of the respective covariates.
3De Boor (2001, Section XIV, (23) Lemma) actually states that our condition of interest, i.e., any
B-spline function having at least one observation in its support, is equivalent to a function ∥· ∥2 on
the span of the B-spline basis, which De Boor (2001) denote with $k,t (where k is the order of the
B-spline basis and t corresponds to the knot sequence), being a norm on $k,t. However, ∥· ∥2 being
a norm on $k,t implies that BX,j has full column rank, which is stated as part of De Boor (2001,
Section XIV, (25) Lemma): “Assume that ∥· ∥2 is a norm on $k,t (that is, rank A = n) [...]”, where
A corresponds to our matrix BX,j and n corresponds to the number of B-spline functions, ultimately
yielding the referenced statement.
5


## Page 31


j ∈{1, . . . , J} and yields linear independence of columns in the concatenated
matrix across different partial effects (BX,j : BX,j′) for j ̸= j′ ∈{1, . . . , J}.
All in all, the full column rank condition and thus assumption (BX) is not re-
strictive and actually requiring full column rank of the design matrix is very
common for regression models. Furthermore, note that (BX) is similar to the
nonsingular-condition for logspline regression models (e.g., Stone, 1991).
2) We will show in Lemma A.5 2) that assumption (BX”) implies (BX’), which
implies (BX) for N ≥N ′. Assumptions (BX’) and (BX”) generalize the lower
bounds of Equations (1) and (2) in Stone (1991), where only one continuous
covariate is considered and no proof is provided for the implication of the first
assumption by the second one. We simplify the second assumption, demanding it
to hold for one α ∈(0, 1), while the corresponding assumption (2) in Stone (1991)
is formulated to hold for all α > 0 (with constants M ′′ and N ′′ depending on α),
which is not necessary for the implication of (BX’).4 While some statements only
assume (BX’) (e.g., Theorem 2.2), this assumption is not very tangible. However,
assumption (BX”) is: It basically requires that the covariate observations are
spread reasonably over the whole domain, i.e., the fraction of observations in large
enough subregions is lower-bounded by a function of the subregion’s volume. In
particular, assumption (BX”) also implies that for any xD ∈X D, the number of
observations taking this particular xD grows with N.
3) Assumption (S<) is always satisfiable under assumption (BX), as we will show
in Lemma A.3 that for all v ∈SK−1 there exists an i ∈{1, . . . , N} such that
the function sv,xi is not constant. Thus, there exists a subset of Y, on which
sv,xi does not reach its maximum. We will furthermore show in Lemma A.5 3)
that under assumption (BX”), the probability that (S<) holds tends to one for
N →∞.
4) We will show in Lemma A.5 4) that assumption (BU) is fulfilled, if ( ˜B(l)
Y : 1Γ(l)) ∈
RΓ(l)×(KY+1) has rank KY + 1 for any l ∈{1, . . . , L}, which for ˜bY chosen as in
Section 2.2 is fulfilled for small enough bin width.

Lemma A.4. The function SK−1 × X →R, (v, x) 7→smax
v,x is (uniformly) continuous.
Proof. Let ε > 0 and denote the maximum norm, defined as the maximum of the
absolute values of the entries of a vector, by ∥· ∥max.
We show that there exists
δ > 0 such that for all (v, x), (v′, x′) ∈SK−1 × X, we have |smax
v,x −smax
v′,x′| < ε, if
∥(x, v) −(x′, v′)∥max < δ. For this purpose, we use uniform continuity of sv,x,c(y) on

This probability is hard to compute directly due to SK−1 being uncountable.
Thus, we construct a subset of AN (which will be denoted as ˜AN), whose proba-
bility tends to 1 for N →∞, based on a finite covering of SK−1, which does not
depend on N. More precisely, we define subsets of SK−1 × X × Y, on which ρ
is positive (i.e., fulfilling the condition of AN such that ˜AN ⊂AN holds), whose
projection onto SK−1 yields a finite covering, whose projection onto Y has posi-
tive measure µ, and whose projection onto X allows to apply (BX”). The latter
two properties particularly will help us to prove that the probability of ˜AN tends
to one. To construct those subsets, we first introduce some notation. Denote the
maximum norm, defined as the maximum of the absolute values of the entries of
a vector, by ∥· ∥max. For v ∈SK−1, x ∈X, y ∈Y, and ε > 0, set
υε(v, x, y) := {(˜v, ˜x, ˜y) ∈SK−1 × X × Y | ∥(˜v, ˜x, ˜y) −(v, x, y)∥max < ε}.
Analogously, define sets υε(·), where (·) corresponds to a sub-tupel of (v, x, y)
e.g., υε(v) := {˜v ∈SK−1 | ∥˜v −v∥max < ε}.

that ˜b(xi)⊤v = sv,xi is not constant. As by 2) there exist observations fulfill-

To define the desired set ˜AN ⊂AN, we first construct a subset of SK−1 × X × Y
containing {(v, xv, yv)} and fulfilling the properties mentioned above. For this

have µ({yv}) = δ({yv}) > 0 and thus can use {yv} directly. Note that in this
case, sv,xv is not continuous in yv and thus the construction from the first case is
not applicable, making separate treatment of the cases necessary. Nevertheless,

exists an εv > 0, such that for all (˜v, ˜x) ∈υεv(v, xv), we have ρ(˜v, ˜x, yv) > 0.
Set Υv := υεv(xv) × {yv} for yv ∈Yd.

A.1.4
Auxiliary statements used in multiple proofs in Section A.2
Before we start formulating and proving the auxiliary statements, we give a brief
overview.
In Proposition A.6, we show negative (positive) definiteness of the Hes-
sian matrices (penalized Fisher informations) of ℓpen and ℓmn
Z,pen, which also implies
invertibility of those matrices. Lemma A.7 states the technical result that matrix in-
version is a continuous function. The remaining theorems, A.8, A.9 and A.10 actually
form a cascade of statements: We derive a generalization of Laplace’s approximation in
Theorem A.8, which is used to show lim∥θ∥2→∞ℓpen(θ) = −∞in Theorem A.9, which
helps us to prove that −ℓpen is level-bounded in Theorem A.10. Ultimately, of these
last three theorems in this section, only Theorem A.10 is referred to in the proofs in
Section A.2.
Denote the (multivariate) expectation with respect to the conditional distributions
Yi | xi, i = 1, . . . , N, with E and the (multivariate) expectation with respect to the
corresponding multinomial distributions with respect to partitions Z with Emn
Z .

matrices H(θ) and Hmn
Z (θ) are negative definite, since a sum of a negative definite and
a negative semi-definite matrix is negative definite and in particular invertible. The
statements for the penalized Fisher informations follows immediately.

Theorem A.10. Under assumption (S<), the function −ℓpen is level-bounded, i.e., for
every α ∈R, the set {θ ∈RK | −ℓpen(θ) ≤α} is bounded.
Proof. Assume −ℓpen is not level-bounded. Then, there exists α ∈R, such that the
set {θ ∈RK | −ℓpen(θ) ≤α} is not bounded. Thus, there exist θ(t) ∈{θ ∈RK | −
ℓpen(θ) ≤α}, t ∈N, with limt→∞∥θ(t)∥2 = ∞. However, we have lim∥θ∥2→∞ℓpen(θ) =
−∞by Theorem A.9, since assumption (S<) holds. Thus, limt→∞−ℓpen(θ(t)) = ∞,
which is a contradiction to −ℓpen(θ(t)) ≤α for all t ∈N. Thus, −ℓpen is level-bounded
and ˆθ exists.
A.2
Proofs of Theorems and Lemmas from the Main Manu-
script (and Formulation and Proof of Lemma A.15)
Proof of Theorem 2.1. We first show uniqueness of the PMLE. From Proposition A.6
1), we have that the Hessian matrix of ℓpen(θ) is negative definite, which implies that
ℓpen(θ) is strictly concave on RK. Per assumption, there exists a PMLE ˆθ. Now, assume
that it is not unique, i.e., there are ˆθ1 ̸= ˆθ2 ∈RK such that ℓpen(ˆθ1) = ℓpen(ˆθ2) =
maxθ∈RK ℓpen(θ). Due to the strict concavity of ℓpen, we have ℓpen((1 −α) ˆθ1 + α ˆθ2) >
(1 −α) ℓpen(ˆθ1) + α ℓpen(ˆθ2) = maxθ∈RK ℓpen(θ) for α ∈(0, 1), which is a contradiction,
i.e., the PMLE is unique, if it exists.
To obtain existence of the PMLE ˆθ (which is also the minimizer of −ℓpen) it suffices to
show that −ℓpen is lower semicontinuous, level-bounded and proper (Rockafellar and
Wets, 2009, Theorem 1.9). As −ℓpen takes values in R, it is proper. Furthermore, it is
continuous, implying it is lower semicontinuous (Rockafellar and Wets, 2009, Exercise
1.12). Finally, by Theorem A.10, −ℓpen is level-bounded and thus ˆθ exists.
Proof of Theorem 2.2
The following auxiliary Lemmas A.11, A.12, and A.14 are dedicated to ultimately prove
consistency and asymptotic normal distribution of the PMLE ˆθ, i.e., Theorem 2.2.
Both of these properties are well-known for “regular log-likelihoods”. However, this
term is often not specified further in the literature, especially in our case of non-
identically distributed (but independent) observations. Exceptions are Hoadley (1971)
and Philippou and Roussas (1975) who both discuss concrete conditions in this case for
asymptotic normality of the unpenalized MLE (Hoadley, 1971, also for consistency),
however, without obtaining the result of asymptotic existence. Furthermore, one con-
dition of Philippou and Roussas (1975) is consistency, whose proof already requires
some ideas needed to show asymptotic normality as well. Our proof generalizes the
one of Cram´er (1946, Section 33.3) for a scalar parameter in the i.i.d. case for unpenal-
ized estimation to a parameter vector in the i.non-i.d. case for penalized estimation.
This includes replacing the weak Law of Large Numbers (Khintchine’s Theorem) and
27


## Page 53


the (Lindeberg-L´evy) Central Limit Theorem with variants not requiring identically
distributed data and including the penalty term, as well as using a multivariate gener-
alization of the intermediate value theorem.

parameter transformation as proposed by Palmgren (1981).

Remark A.17.
1) Distributional assumption 1) “only” yields equal results with
respect to θ. By adjusting the Poisson rates via weights in 2), we obtain the same
estimation results with respect to the whole vector of unknown parameters (θ, τ).
As we are only interested in estimating θ, it is sufficient to use distributional
assumption 1).
2) By Theorem 2.6, the estimation results with respect to θ obtained by both dis-
tributional assumptions 1) and 2) on n(l)
g r are also identical to the ones obtained
by the multinomial distributional assumption on n(l) = (n(l)
1 , . . . , n(l)
Γ(l)) ∈NΓ(l) as
in Theorem 2.6 2).
3) In the most extreme case, ρ(l) = n(l) for all l = 1, . . . , L, the construction in
the lemma yields one vector of counts per observation (with one count of 1 and
all others 0). While this is usually not desirable with respect to computation
time in practice, since it inflates the size of the data set artificially, it shows the
estimation does not depend on aggregation of observations and the presented
approach is thus overall coherent.

B
Transforming effects to desired reference coding
The function densreg() used for fitting density regression models contained in our
R package DensityRegression (developer version on https://github.com/Eva2703/
DensityRegression) uses the function gam() of the well-known package mgcv (Wood,
2017) for Poisson regression. To the best of the author’s knowledge, the reference coding
used in Maier et al. (2025) cannot be specified directly using mgcv::gam(), since it
does not seem intended to specify a reference value for a continuous covariate like year.
Thus, also the reference year cannot be specified directly in densreg(). Instead, we
transform the received estimated effects, appropriately. The model actually estimated
via densreg() is
fWest East, c age, year = ˜β0 ⊕˜βWest East ⊕˜βc age ⊕˜βc age, West East
⊕˜g0(year) ⊕˜gWest East(year) ⊕˜gc age(year)
⊕˜gc age, West East(year),
(B.1)
with reference categories West for West East and other for c age.14 The effects as dis-
cussed in Section D, i.e., with reference category 1991 for year, are obtained by adding
14Regarding the interaction effects for West East and c age, note that it is not possible to use the
argument by of the smoother mgcv::ti() with more than one variable. Thus, we combine c age
and West East in a new covariate for the corresponding interaction terms, where all combinations
containing either West East = West or c age = other are set to the same (reference) category, such
that they all have an effect of zero, in accordance with the reference coding.
50


## Page 76


the respective smooth effect for 1991 for (group specific) intercepts and subtracting it
for (group specific) smooth effects:
β0 = ˜β0 ⊕˜g0(1991)
βWest East = ˜βWest East ⊕˜gWest East(1991)
βc age = ˜βc age ⊕˜gc age(1991)
βc age, West East = ˜βc age, West East ⊕˜gc age, West East(1991)
g0(year) = ˜g0(year) ⊖˜g0(1991)
gWest East(year) = ˜gWest East(year) ⊖˜gWest East(1991)
gc age(year) = ˜gc age(year) ⊖˜gc age(1991)
gc age, West East(year) = ˜gc age, West East(year) ⊖˜gc age, West East(1991)
and the resulting model is equivalent to model (B.1), i.e.,
fWest East, c age, year = β0 ⊕βWest East ⊕βc age ⊕βc age, West East
⊕g0(year) ⊕gWest East(year) ⊕gc age(year)
⊕gc age, West East(year)
= ˜β0 ⊕˜βWest East ⊕˜βc age ⊕˜βc age, West East
⊕˜g0(year) ⊕˜gWest East(year) ⊕˜gc age(year)
⊕˜gc age, West East(year).
To obtain confidence regions for the transformed effects, note that all transformations
are of the form ˆh = L

C
Including observation weights
The SOEP data analyzed in Section D contains sampling weights for all individual
observations. Denote the sampling weight of the i-th observation with γi, i = 1, . . . , N.
They would reasonably be included by replacing the counts n(l)
g
in Section 2.3 with
weighted counts, i.e., setting n(l)
g
:= P
i∈I(l)

g (yi) for g = 1, . . . , Γ(l), l = 1, . . . , L,

this is not feasible, since the weights γi included in the SOEP data are not natural
numbers, but positive real numbers.15
Thus, also the weighted counts n(l)
g
are not
natural numbers and theoretically cannot serve as response observations for a Poisson
model. Consequently, trying to estimate such a model via the function gam() of the R
package mgcv (Wood, 2017), which our newly developed R package DensityRegression
uses to fit Poisson models, produces an error.
In the following, we thus present a
15Actually, there are even weights of exactly zero. The corresponding observations were removed
from the data set, with 151, 838 observations having positive real weights remaining.
51


## Page 77


work-around, which yields the desired likelihood of a weighted counts Poisson model,
but is specifiable in gam().
For this purpose, we use the (unweighted) counts n(l)
g
as defined in Section 2.3 as observed response values, but appropriately weight the
log-likelihood contributions (specifiable via the gam()-argument weights) and include
offsets (specifiable in gam() via an offset in the formula of the model) that cancel
the undesired terms. In the function densreg(), which is used to fit regression models
with our approach in our R package DensityRegression, the sample weights γ1, . . . , γN
can be specified via the argument sample weights. The weights for the log-likelihood
contributions and the offset are then constructed appropriately by densreg().

D
Application
In this section, we provide further material related to the application presented in
Section D.
D.1
Smoothing Parameters
Table D.1 shows the smoothing parameters (rounded to three digits) corresponding
to the (group-specific) smooth effects – modeled via cubic B-splines with second order
difference penalty – obtained by REML optimization (Wood, 2017, Section 6.5.2). Note
that for all unpenalized marginal basis functions (i.e., bY and all basis functions bj
modeling non-smooth covariate effects), the corresponding smoothing parameters are
zero and not listed in the table.

D.2
Significance and p-values
Table D.2 shows the p-values (rounded to three digits) for the null hypothesis that a
partial effects equals zero as in Lemma A.15, which are simultaneous in the covari-
ates.16 For most effects, the obtained p-values are smaller than 0.01 and in particular
significant for the significance level α = 5%. The only two exceptions are the smooth
interaction effects of year with West East and of year with c age and West East, whose
p-values are very large with values close to 1.

D.3
Estimated effects
This section contains all estimated effects of model (3.1) with a sample of their 95%
confidence region (sample size: 100). They are structured similar to Figures 3.1 to 3.3,
however with one panel per covariate value to avoid overplotting.
For interaction
effects, we display the sum of the intercept, the respective effect, and the corresponding
main effects for the estimated conditional densities (left side of the figures). Note that

the estimated functions, cutting off some of the functions sampled from the confidence
regions.

Figure D.16: Estimated conditional densities [upper 2 × 3 panels] and corresponding
relative frequencies [lower 2×3 panels] for West vs. East Germany [rows] and all three
values of c age [columns].
Figure D.16 shows the estimated conditional densities obtained from model (3.1) for
the different combinations of covariate values in the upper part. The different years
are illustrated in one panel, respectively, distinguishable from each other via a color
gradient and different line types. For the discrete values with positive probability mass,
0 and 1, the density values are visualized as dashes and shifted slightly outwards for
better distinction. Their smoothly estimated development over time, as well as the
one of dual-earner households (corresponding to the Lebesgue integrals of the densi-
ties), is shown explicitly in the lower part of the figure. We briefly point out some
interesting results visible in this figure. In West Germany in all three categories of
c age, the share of nonworking women (s = 0) is decreasing over time while the share
of dual-earner households (s ∈(0, 1)) is increasing. Simultaneously, the probability
mass for lower positive income shares increases, indicating an increase of previously
nonworking women in part-time employment. Without considering mixed densities
for single- and double-earner couples, but only continuous densities for double-earner
couples on (0, 1), this might be misinterpreted as a shift from larger to smaller income
shares, nicely showing the necessity of considering mixed densities. Furthermore, there
is more probability mass at lower positive income shares for couples with minor children
(0-6 and 7-18) compared to those without minor children (other) in West Germany,
reflecting that women are rather working part-time in the presence of children. In East
Germany the shapes of the densities are less dependent on the age of the youngest
child. Especially for couples without minor children (other), the densities are close to
symmetric with a mode around s = 0.45. For couples with minor children (0-6 and
7-18), this is only the case for the earlier years, while over time, probability mass tends
70


## Page 96


to shift towards smaller income shares. Again, this might include previously nonwork-
ing women switching to part-time employment even though there is not such a clear
decline of nonworking women as in West Germany. Actually, for couples with small
children (0-6), the frequency of nonworking women even increases from 1991 to 1998 in
East Germany, reaching a similar level as in West Germany, before decreasing again.
All in all, accompanying the findings of Maier et al. (2025), our observations reflect
the different social norms established in the two parts of Germany before reunification
in 1990: In East Germany, comprehensive child care was available and women were
expected to work, while in West Germany, it was more common for mothers to be
unemployed and instead take care of the children themselves.
E
Simulation study
This section contains further figures related to the simulation study presented in Sec-
tion 4.
Figure E.1 is the extended version of Figure 4.1, including interaction ef-
fects. Figure E.2 shows the empirical coverage rates for all partial effects based on
Lemma A.15, i.e., simultaneous in covariates. Compared to Figure 4.2, interaction ef-
fects are added, while excluding prediction. Instead, in this section, relMSE( ˆf), which
is computed based on Lemma 2.3, is shown in Figure E.3 together with the empirical
coverage rates for all partial effects, also based on Lemma 2.3, i.e., point-wise in the
covariates, since conceptually they belong together.
